● All 12 tracks are closed and merged. `main` is clean and matches `origin/main`, the typecheck passes, and all 171 tests pass. What's left is the work no track covers, plus the items tracks deferred.

  ## Status

  - **Backend:** Lean, recognition and translation run as containers on EC2 (Sydney). Retrieval is a Python package inside translation.
  - **Frontend:** the Vercel site proxies Lean, recognition and translation, and runs feedback itself against Gemini.
  - **Typed end-to-end run:** 5 of 8 test proofs close with the original wording. With student-style wording the three proofs tried (tp-01 to tp-03) close.
  - **Not yet run:** real handwriting through the whole loop. Only synthetic ink has been tested, and neither of those two proofs closed.

  ## Pending work no track covers

  1. **Real-handwriting end-to-end run.** You deferred it to the end. You draw 4–5 test proofs, then we run `e2e.ts --ink` on them. This is also the first real test of the structure parser, whose thresholds were tuned on synthetic boxes only.
  2. **Who can use the app, and abuse protection.** `plan.md` leans toward invite-code access, but nothing is built. The Vercel `/api/*` functions are open, so anyone can spend your Gemini free quota and hit EC2. Quota exhaustion already caused "translator busy" errors in testing.
  3. **Rotate the Lean API key and set the AWS budget alert.** Both are unticked in `docs/deployment.md`. The key was pasted into a chat during setup, and the budget alert matters because the EC2 credits are finite.
  4. **Backend ownership and logging.** `SPEC.md` still lists "backend scaffold and shared logging" as open. Track 8 hasn't confirmed the `request_id` log convention. The data is meant to seed the research track.
  5. **Edited-earlier-step policy.** Open in `SPEC.md`: when a student edits or redraws an earlier step, do later steps re-run, go stale, or get discarded? Today one failed step blocks the rest of the proof.
  6. **Student-style wording is weak.** Translation closes 3 of 3 on tp-01 to tp-03 but not tp-04 (sums) or tp-08. Multi-claim steps (tp-06) are refused. Invented lemma names still occur, because retrieval returns noise for step-text queries.
  7. **Feedback evaluation never run.** The 20+ case set was never supplied, so the review pass bar (all true to Lean, 90% on the other criteria) is unmeasured. The "bubble lines are generic" worry is also unchecked.
  8. **Calibration accuracy is unmeasured.** `calibration-eval.ts` hasn't run on your samples, the live server hasn't been confirmed to accept samples without `t`, and stylus and touch are untested.
  9. **Proof persistence and export.** The canvas has `persist.ts`, but I haven't verified a reload restores a proof. I found no export of the finished Lean file, which `SPEC.md` describes as the skeleton ending in `sorry`.
  10. **No CI, and the README is junk.** `README.md` holds only a stray `claude --resume` line. There is no `.github` or other CI, and no user-facing "what is this / how to run it" doc.
  11. **Leftover cleanup.**
      - The `track/canvas`, `track/retrieval` and `track/translation` branches still exist.
      - The test calls (`claude-test-*`, `claude-live-*`) are still in `/data/attempts.jsonl` on EC2.
      - `app/dist` and `app/shots` are in the working tree.
      - The CRLF line endings make prettier flag nearly every file.
  12. **Words in proofs ("assume", "therefore").** Decided as out of V1, but untested, and the Gemini line reader might already handle short words.

Response
Here is everything left in the project, in plain words. I've grouped it by what it's about.

  ## A. Things only you can do
  1. **Draw 4–5 proofs by hand and run the whole loop on them.** So far the full pipeline has only been tested with typed steps and computer-drawn "fake" handwriting. Your real handwriting is the true test of reading, parsing, translating and checking together. You wanted this at the very end.
  2. **Give me your handwriting samples (the calibration screen) and the recognition key.** I can then measure whether calibration improves reading. Right now we don't know if it helps at all.
  3. **Test the calibration screen with a stylus or touch.** It has only been tested with synthetic mouse events.
  4. **Get a second person to look at the app.** They tell us whether it looks like its own product or a generic one.
  5. **Try the "signature moment" with your real ink.** This is the companion's eyes turning to the step you just wrote, plus the highlight. It has only been seen with fake ink.
  6. **Supply 20+ test cases for the feedback messages** (or let me build them from the test proofs). Then someone scores whether the explanations are actually useful. This check was never done.
  7. **Optional: get a Mistral key** to compare a second translation model (Leanstral).

  ## B. Safety and money
  8. **Rotate the Lean API key.** The current one was pasted into a chat.
  9. **Set an AWS budget alert**, so the free credits don't run out unnoticed.
  10. **Decide who may use the app.** Today anyone with the link can use the backends and drain your free Gemini quota. The plan's favourite option is an invite code that the servers check.
  11. **Use a paid or safer Gemini setup if the app goes public.** The free tier already ran out mid-testing and caused "translator busy" errors.

  ## C. Product behaviour still undecided or weak
  12. **Editing an earlier step.** If a student changes step 2, what happens to steps 3, 4 and 5? Options are re-check them, mark them stale, or discard them. Not decided.
  13. **One bad step blocks the whole proof.** Once a step fails, later steps wait until it's fixed. Simple, but harsh, so we may want a softer rule.
  14. **Translation quality on how students really write.** Students write "a + b = 2j + 2k" and "So n² is even", not instructions like "Replace a with 2j". Those are much harder, and tp-04 (sums) and tp-08 still fail.
  15. **Steps with two claims in one sentence** (tp-06) are refused. Earlier they compiled into a wrong tactic, which is worse.
  16. **Made-up lemma names.** The translator sometimes invents a Mathlib name that doesn't exist (tp-05 did this). Retrieval gives poor results for step text, so it doesn't catch these.
  17. **Translations that compile but say something different from what the student meant.** Examples are `Even a` instead of `∃ k, n = 2*k`, and wrong tactics. Nothing yet checks that the Lean matches the student's meaning (faithfulness). This is also the planned research track.
  18. **English words in a line** ("assume", "therefore"). This was left out of V1. It may already work with the Gemini reader, but it's untested.
  19. **Retrieval re-check.** Re-run the lemma search test on the real test proofs and on recognised (not typed) text. Also measure whether the lemma shortlist actually helps translation.
  20. **Structure parser limits.** Its position thresholds were tuned on fake boxes, and real ink might break it. Later we also need to decide what to do when the ink and the text reading disagree ("ambiguous").

  ## D. Missing or unverified features
  21. **Saving and reloading a proof.** I haven't confirmed that a reload brings the proof back. I need to check, and build it if it doesn't.
  22. **Exporting the finished Lean file.** The spec describes it, but I found no export in the app.
  23. **Real-world error and edge handling.** Examples are the server being asleep, a Lean timeout, and Gemini quota errors shown to the student. The client does retry "busy", but I haven't audited the rest.
  24. **Per-step log for the research track.** Every step should be recorded (informal text, Lean version, whether it compiled). The format is proposed, but track 8 hasn't confirmed it and no shared place collects the logs. Today they only sit in Docker logs and Vercel logs.

  ## E. Things deliberately left out of V1 (add only if real use needs them)
  25. **Select/lasso tool** to move and resize ink. This needs the step model to follow moved ink first.
  26. **Live collaboration**, so a teacher can watch a proof.
  27. **Shareable read-only links.**
  28. **LaTeX as a second way to type a step.**
  29. **Feedback shown on the canvas next to the ink**, not only in the side panel.
  30. **Screenshot pass for the typed-step dialog.** It was missed in the polish pass.
  31. **Phones and tablets.** Layout is only checked down to laptop size.
  32. **Fine-tuning** the translator, and the research track after V1. Both are stretch goals.

  ## F. Housekeeping
  33. **Write a real README.** The current one is a stray `claude --resume` line.
  34. **Add automatic tests on every push (CI).** Today the 171 tests only run when someone runs them.
  35. **Delete old branches and clean up.**
      - Old branches `track/canvas`, `track/retrieval` and `track/translation`.
      - Test entries (`claude-test-*`) in the server's attempts log.
      - The `shots` folder and the built `dist` folder.
  36. **Fix the line-ending noise.** Windows CRLF makes the formatter flag almost every file.
  37. **Tidy the specs.** Some old checkboxes in `SPEC/03` and `SPEC/10` are still unticked even though the work was done. Some memory notes are outdated too, for example one saying the feedback work isn't committed.
  38. **Tie up loose contracts.** `symbol-schema.md` needs review by tracks 3, 7, 8 and 10. `translation-interface.md` is still marked DRAFT.

  ## What I can do now without you
  Items 8–9 need your AWS and Vercel access, but I can prepare the steps. I can do items 21–24 (check or build saving, export, error handling and logging) and 33–38 (README, CI, cleanup, spec tidy) myself. I can also draft the invite-code gate (10) and a stale-step rule (12) for you to approve.

  Do you want me to start with 21–24 and 33–38, then propose designs for 10 and 12?

  ## Done (branch `fix/recheck-names-words`, not merged or deployed yet)

  **12 – Recheck later steps.** When a step is edited, redrawn or erased, the steps after it are cleared and re-run in order (translate, Lean check, review). They wait for the changed step to finish first. This also fixes a smaller problem: a quick later step no longer fails with "step N has no Lean yet" and stays failed. It has unit tests (178 pass, typecheck clean). I haven't tried it in a browser.

  **16 – Invented lemma names.**
  - The translator was retrying nothing when Lean rejected a step, so a made-up name went straight to the student.
  - It now gets one repair attempt. Lean's error is sent back to the model together with the closest real Mathlib names.
  - For example, `Nat.odd_iff_not_even` now points to `Nat.not_even_iff_odd`.
  - It costs a Gemini call only when Lean rejects a step.

  **18 – Words in a line.** The handwriting reader's instructions now say to expect and copy words like "Assume", "Since" and "Therefore". The rest of the pipeline already passes words through. The translator already has English examples, so I expect this to work, but I haven't tested it.

  ## Blocked: the server is down
  Both EC2 health URLs time out, and SSH times out too. The instance is probably stopped, or the security group's SSH rule is for an old IP. Your current public IP is `14.139.128.47`. Please start the instance in the AWS console and add that IP to the SSH rule. Then I can:
  - Run the live translation checks for 14, 15 and 17, and tune the prompt.
  - Do item 19, the retrieval re-check.
  - Deploy the translation changes, then test words with synthetic handwriting.

  ## Plans for the rest (needs the server)
  - **14 – student-style wording.** Add more worked examples of equation-style and "So…" lines (sums, mod 4) to the translator's examples. Then re-run `eval.py --student` and keep what helps.
  - **15 – two claims in one sentence.** Let the translator output several short tactics (one per line) when a sentence states two facts. Today it refuses or picks one. I'd test this on tp-06.
  - **17 – compiles but means something else.** The cheap fix is to make the translator write each claim as `have h : <claim> := …`, so the student sees what was checked, and to compare the numbers and letters in the student's line with the Lean. The full fix is a separate checker, which is the planned research track. I'd do the cheap version now.

  ## 20, in simple words
  The structure parser takes the typed-out line, such as `x^2 + 1 = 5`, and builds a tree of what is added to what. It also looks at where each handwritten symbol sits on the page. If the text says "2 raised to a power" but the ink shows the 2 written on the baseline, the two disagree. The tool then marks the line "ambiguous", and the open question is what to do about that. We could ask the student "did you mean x² or x2?", or trust the text and carry on. Right now it trusts the text. This only matters with real handwriting, so I'd leave it until you've drawn your proofs.

  Tell me when the server is up and I'll continue.

  ## What changed

  - **12 – recheck later steps.** Editing, redrawing or erasing a step now re-runs the steps after it, in order. I haven't tried it in a browser, only in unit tests.

  - **15 – several reasons in one sentence.**
    - New worked examples (`fs-18a`, `fs-18b`, `fs-18c`) and a prompt rule let the translator write multi-line answers, a chained term or one `have` per reason.

  - **17 – compiles but means something else.** A line with `=` that is "translated" as a bare `ring` or `simp` closing nothing is now sent back once to be written as a `have`.     - `GEMINI_TIMEOUT_S` sets how long to wait for the model.

● Everything below is for the live app. It's what you need for the handwriting run, calibration, the second reviewer and the feedback evaluation. Before any of it, start the EC2 instance and wait about 2 minutes for Lean to load. Check `https://54-252-156-144.sslip.io/health`; it should say `"ready":true`. Keep your Gemini quota in mind: heavy testing can exhaust the free tier, which shows up as "translator busy".

  ## A. The real-handwriting run (the most important one)

  1. **Setup.**
     - Open the deployed site, using the same Vercel production URL you already use.
     - Use the device you'll actually write on, ideally a tablet with a stylus.
     - Do the handwriting setup first (section B).
  2. **Pick the proofs.** The test proofs are in `content/test-proofs.json` (original wording) and `content/test-proofs-student.json` (equation-style lines, closer to how students write). Start with these four and write them in your own way:
     - tp-01, sum of evens
     - tp-02, square of an even
     - tp-03, divisibility
     - tp-05, square even implies even
     - Add tp-04 (sums) if you want a harder one.
     - The first line you write is the statement, for example "If n is even, then n² is even."
  3. **Write one step per line.**
     - Leave a clear gap between lines.
     - When you start the next line, the previous one is read. For the last line, wait about 10 seconds.
     - If a symbol is uncertain, a "Read as … Correct?" prompt appears. Press Correct, or Rewrite and redraw it.
     - Click the face in the top-right corner to open the step panel.
  4. **Record this for each line.** Use a simple table, or a notes file:

     | proof | line | what the panel read | right? | status shown | Lean text | explanation useful? |
     |---|---|---|---|---|---|---|

     - **Read right?** compares the reading with what you wrote.
     - **Status** is verified, incomplete or error.
     - **Explanation** is yes or no, with a note.
  5. **Also try these deliberately:**
     - Redraw a symbol, to check the rewrite flow.
     - Erase part of a line.
     - Edit an earlier line after writing later ones. The later steps should re-check by themselves.
     - Write a line with words ("Assume n is even.") and one wrong step, to see the error explanation.
  6. **Tell me what you found.** Send me the table, or just the failures. I'll sort each one by stage: reading, structure, translation, Lean or explanation. Those results are what decide what we fix next.

  ## B. Calibration and stylus

  1. Open the menu, choose "Handwriting setup", and draw the 12 symbols 3 times each. Do it with the stylus on the tablet.
  2. **Stylus check.** Note any problems: strokes not drawn, a palm drawing marks, boxes too small.
  3. **Accuracy check, optional.** In the browser dev console on that device, run: `copy(JSON.stringify(JSON.parse(localStorage.getItem('proofcal.samples')).samples))` and paste the result into a file `samples.json`. Then, in the `app` folder:
     ```
     RECOGNITION_API_URL=https://recognition.54-252-156-144.sslip.io RECOGNITION_API_KEY=<key> node scripts/calibration-eval.ts samples.json
     ```
     - The key is in `~/.recognition.env` on the server. Type `! ssh -i ~/.ssh/proof-verifier-key.pem ec2-user@[ip] "grep RECOGNITION_API_KEY ~/.recognition.env"` in the prompt to print it. Don't paste it anywhere else.
     - It reports how often the reading is right with and without your samples. For a fair test, do a second round of setup into another file and pass it as the second argument.

  ## C. A second reviewer
  Ask one person who hasn't seen the app to write one short proof on it with no help from you. Then ask only: "does this feel like a product with its own character, or like a generic app?" and "what confused you?" Watch silently and write down where they hesitate.

  ## D. Feedback evaluation
  1. **Cases.** You need 20+ real Lean results. The easiest way is to let me build them from the test-proof runs, which cover correct, incomplete and wrong steps. Tell me when, and I'll produce `cases.json` and the scoring sheet.
  2. **Scoring.** Open the sheet and give each explanation 0 or 1 on these five points (the rubric is in `docs/feedback-eval.md`):
     - true to Lean
     - specific
     - actionable
     - tone
     - fits the bubble
  3. **Pass bar.** Every explanation must be true to Lean, at least 90% on the rest, and at most 20% may fall back to the fixed template.

  ## E. Re-measure the translator
  When the Gemini quota has reset, I run the full `eval.py` on all test proofs and the student set. You don't need to do anything for this beyond starting the instance and telling me.

  If you want, I can also build a small script that exports what you wrote on the canvas into the format my test harness reads. Then the same handwritten proofs could be re-run every time we change the translator.

Turn 29
for the remaining thigns tell me step by step how do i test the app
Response
The evaluation is still running in the background, and I'll report the numbers when it finishes. Here is the step-by-step version of the remaining tests.

  ## Step 0: Get ready (5 minutes)
  1. The EC2 instance is already running. Check that `https://54-252-156-144.sslip.io/health` shows `"ready":true`.
  2. Open the deployed app (the Vercel production URL) on the device you'll write on, ideally a tablet with a stylus. A laptop with a mouse also works for a first pass.
  3. Open `content/test-proofs.json` next to it, so you have proofs to copy.

  ## Step 1: Handwriting setup and stylus check
  1. Open the menu and choose **Handwriting setup**.
  2. Draw each of the 12 symbols 3 times with the stylus.
  3. Note anything odd, such as:
     - strokes that don't appear
     - your palm drawing marks
     - boxes that are too small
  4. Optional accuracy check: in the browser dev console run this, and save the output to `samples.json`:
     ```
     copy(JSON.stringify(JSON.parse(localStorage.getItem('proofcal.samples')).samples))
     ```
     Then send me the file. I'll run the calibration scoring, since the key stays on my side.

  ## Step 2: Write 4 or 5 real proofs
  1. Take them from the test list: tp-01 (sum of evens), tp-02 (square of an even), tp-03 (divisibility), tp-05 (square even implies even), and tp-04 if you want a harder one.
  2. Write the statement first, for example "If n is even, then n² is even."
  3. Then write one step per line, with a clear gap between lines.
  4. Wait about 10 seconds after the last line.
  5. If a "Read as … Correct?" prompt appears, press **Correct**, or **Rewrite** and redraw the symbol.
  6. Click the face in the top-right corner to open the step panel, and look at each line.

  ## Step 3: Record what you see
  Keep a simple table, one row per line:

  | proof | line | what the panel read | right? | status | explanation useful? |
  |---|---|---|---|---|---|

  Send me the table, or only the rows that went wrong. I'll sort each failure by stage (reading, structure, translation, Lean or explanation).

  ## Step 4: Try the awkward cases on purpose
  1. Redraw a symbol, to check the rewrite flow.
  2. Erase part of a line.
  3. Edit an earlier line after writing later ones. The later steps should say "Checking…" and re-run by themselves.
  4. Write a line with words ("Assume n is even.").
  5. Write one wrong step, to see the error explanation.

  ## Step 5: Second person
  1. Ask someone who has never seen the app to write one short proof with no help from you.
  2. Watch silently and note where they hesitate.
  3. Ask only two things: "Does this feel like it has its own character, or like a generic app?" and "What confused you?"

  ## Step 6: Feedback scoring
  1. Once Step 2 is done, tell me. I'll build 20+ cases from your runs and a scoring sheet.
  2. For each explanation, give 0 or 1 on five points: true to Lean, specific, actionable, tone, fits the bubble.
  3. The pass bar is every explanation true to Lean, at least 90% on the rest, and at most 20% falling back to the fixed template.

  ## Not tests, but decisions for you
  - An AWS budget alert (a few minutes in the AWS console).
  - Who may use the app (an invite code), and whether to move to a paid Gemini key.

  Do Steps 1 to 3 first, because they decide what we fix next.

● Yes, I've made it work that way, and it isn't committed yet.

  ## What changes
  - **Order follows the page.** A new line is placed by where it sits vertically, not by when it was written.
  - **A line written between two others** becomes the step between them. The lines below it get new numbers.
  - **A line written above the first one** becomes the Statement. The old statement becomes Step 1 and is translated again as a step.
  - **Lines below an insertion re-translate and re-check one at a time, top to bottom.** Their handwriting readings are kept, so nothing is re-read. This uses the same re-check I added earlier for edited lines.
  - **A change to the first line already worked** by re-reading and re-translating it as the statement, so I left that alone.
  - **A typed step with no position** still goes last, as before.
  - **Extra fix:** if a lower line was mid-translation when you inserted above it, its late answer is now dropped. Before, it could have been applied against the old order.

  ## Where it is
  - **Code:** the placing rule is in `app/src/steps/order.ts`, used by the reducer in `stepReducer.ts` and by `useInkCapture.ts`.
  - **Tests:** I added 8 tests in `order.test.ts`, covering the middle, the top, the bottom, typed steps, the re-check order, and a late answer being dropped. All 189 app tests pass, and the type check shows no errors.
  - **Not verified:** I haven't drawn on the canvas to watch it happen. Only the logic is tested, so please try it on your tablet once it's deployed.

  ## Limits
  - **Same-height lines:** lines are compared by their vertical middle, so two lines at the same height are treated as one line, as before.
  - **Growing lines:** a line that grows doesn't move past a neighbour. It only changes position when it is first written.
  - **Unfinished lines:** if you add a line in the middle within seconds of finishing a lower line, that lower line may re-check once more than needed.

  ## Uncommitted work
  - **Ordering change:** the change above.
  - **Statement message:** the clearer Statement-row message.
  - **Reader timeout:** the reader timeout raised from 15 to 40 seconds.
  - **Readable proofs:** `content/test-proofs.md` and its script.

  The timeout fix needs a rebuild of the recognition container on EC2 to take effect. The other changes reach Vercel by themselves once pushed.

  Do you want me to commit all of it, push it, and rebuild the recognition container? I'll leave out the result files and `.claude/launch.json`, and the commit message will have no co-author line.

Everything below runs on your tablet at proof-verifier-one.vercel.app. Reload the page first, so you get the new code.

  ## Before you start
  - The EC2 instance is running. If you stopped it, start it and wait about 2 minutes.
  - Open the step panel (click the face at the top right) and keep it open while you write.
  - Use a fresh page, so old lines don't interfere. Menu → Page → New page.

  ## Test 1: a normal proof, top to bottom
  1. Write the statement on the first line: "If a and b are even, then a + b is even."
  2. Write these lines below it, one per line, with a clear gap between lines:
     - Let a and b be even numbers
     - a = 2j and b = 2k
     - a + b = 2j + 2k
     - a + b = 2(j + k)
     - So a + b is even
  3. After the last line, wait about 30 seconds. The reader may now take up to 40 seconds per try on a slow Gemini day.
  4. Check the panel.
     - **Expect:** a reading for every row, including the Statement and the last row, and the rows going to verified or incomplete rather than error.
     - **If a row still shows error:** tap it and send me the message.

  ## Test 2: a line in the middle
  1. With Test 1 finished, write a new line between "a = 2j and b = 2k" and "a + b = 2j + 2k". For example: "Then a + b = 2j + 2k".
  2. Check the panel.
     - **Expect:** the new line appears as the step between those two, and the later rows are renumbered.
     - **Expect:** the rows below it show "Checking…" and then settle again, one after the other, top to bottom.
     - **Check:** the Lean for each row is still sensible.

  ## Test 3: a line above the first
  1. On a fresh page, write a step line first, such as "Let a and b be even numbers".
  2. Then write the statement above it.
     - **Expect:** the new top line becomes the Statement, and the old one becomes Step 1.

  ## Test 4: a statement that isn't a claim
  1. On a fresh page, write "Let a and b be even" as the first line.
  2. Tap the Statement row.
     - **Expect:** the message "The first line has to say what is to be proved…" and not the old "one claim per line" wording.

  ## What to send me
  For each test, a screenshot of the panel, or a line like "Test 2: new line became Step 2, rows below re-checked, all verified". Send the exact message for anything that shows error or looks wrong. Also tell me:
  - whether Step 2's reading ("a = 2j and b = 2k") is correct this time, because that was misread before;
  - how long the last line took to read.

Turn 47
can i use claude or something instead of gemini?
Response
Yes. All three places that call Gemini could use Claude instead, and I'd expect it to help with the slow, overloaded calls you've been hitting.

  ## Where Gemini is used today
  | Part | What it does | Runs on |
  |---|---|---|
  | Translation | Turns each line into Lean (text only) | EC2 container |
  | Recognition | Reads a whole handwritten line from the picture (and backs up single symbols) | EC2 container |
  | Feedback | Writes the plain-English review of a step | Vercel function |

  ## What it would take
  - **A Claude API key.** You'd create it in the Anthropic console, and the API is pay-per-token with no free tier. I'd never see it: you'd put it in the server's env file (and in Vercel for feedback) yourself, the way the Gemini key is set up now.
  - **Code.** Add a Claude option to each of the three places, chosen by an environment setting. Gemini stays as the fallback or the alternative, so we can switch back if needed.
  - **Models.** Claude Haiku 5.5 would suit the fast, frequent calls (reading a line, writing feedback), and Claude Sonnet 5.5 the translation, which is the hard part. The model IDs are `claude-haiku-5-5` and `claude-sonnet-5-5`.
  - **Time.** About an hour for the three changes, plus a redeploy of the translation and recognition containers and the Vercel app.

  ## What I can't promise
  - **Quality is untested.** I haven't measured Claude on your handwriting or on these proofs. I'd run the same translation evaluation (`eval.py`, both wordings) and your 18-line reading set before switching anything, and compare with Gemini's numbers.
  - **Cost is unknown to me.** It depends on the model and how many lines get read and translated. It will be small per step, but I don't have a figure, so check the pricing page for the current numbers.
  - **Privacy.** Your handwriting images and proof text would go to Anthropic instead of Google.

  ## Other choices
  - **A paid Gemini key** fixes the same problem with no code changes, but you'd still depend on Google being fast.
  - **Mistral's Leanstral** is a proof-focused model, which was the optional comparison you postponed. It's text only, so it would only help the translation, not the reading.

  ## My suggestion
  Start with translation only, since it already has an evaluation to compare against, and it's where the slow calls hurt most. Then add reading and feedback if the numbers hold up.

  Do you want me to go ahead with that? If so, create the API key and tell me when it's in `~/.translation.env` on the server as `ANTHROPIC_API_KEY`, and I'll build the Claude option and run the comparison.
Turn 48
can i use this pro account? would that work?
Response
No, a Claude Pro subscription can't power the app's calls.

  - **Separate billing.** Pro covers claude.ai and Claude Code. The API, which is what the app's servers would call, is a separate product at console.anthropic.com. It has its own prepaid credits and no free tier, and a Pro subscription doesn't give you an API key or any API credit.
  - **No workaround.** I can't point the app at your Pro login. Subscription logins are meant for Anthropic's own apps, not for running another product's backend, so I wouldn't try to route the app through one even if it were technically possible.

  ## What would work
  1. **An API account.** Sign up at console.anthropic.com, add a small amount of credit, and create a key. Each line is a small request, so testing on a few proofs shouldn't use much, but I can't give you an exact figure. Set a low spending limit in the console if you want a safety cap.
  2. **A paid Gemini key.** It costs money too, and it needs no code changes.
  3. **Wait it out.** Gemini slowness comes and goes. You could carry on testing at quieter times, and nothing needs to be built for that.

  Does your university or IISc have API credits, or a research allowance you can use? A project like this is the kind of thing that can sometimes be covered, and it would save you paying yourself.

  Which do you want to do? If you set up an API account, the plan from my last message still applies.

  - **Translation:** one call per step, plus one more when Lean rejects it. This is the biggest cost, since each prompt is long.
  - **Reading a handwritten line:** one call per finished line, with a small image.
  - **Feedback:** one short call, and only for steps that need a written review.

  ## What I'd suggest to protect your $4
  1. **Use Claude (Sonnet) for translation only.** That's where it clearly beats Gemini.
  2. **Keep Gemini as the main reader and feedback writer** while it's free, with Claude only as the backup when Gemini fails or is slow. That means setting the switch the other way round for those two parts.
  3. **Set a spending limit** in the console, at about $4, so it can never go past what you have.
  4. **Skip the handwriting comparison for now.** It was only about 25 small calls, but it isn't needed to decide the above.

  Is that the plan you want? If so, I'll make the reading and feedback change, with no API calls, and ask before committing and deploying.

  4. **Statement that isn't a claim.** On a fresh page write "Let a and b be even" first.
     - **Expect:** the message about the first line needing to say what is to be proved.

  If something fails, send me the exact row message and the time. I can look up which model handled it in the server log.

  ## Things to know
  - **Reading is still on Gemini.** If Gemini is slow again, those reads can still time out. The retry now goes to the other Gemini model, which should help, but it isn't a fix.
  - **Leanstral is a Labs model**, and your free Mistral plan may use inputs to improve its models. That's fine for test proofs.

i was trying to deploy the backend and i was trying to use the aws free credits. so i logged into aws i created an instance of a machine [Image #1] this is where i am rn, tell me what to do next
Response
I'll check what the backend needs so the steps match your project.

● Bash(ls && ls backend* server* api* 2>/dev/null; find . -maxdepth 3 \( -name "Dockerfile*" -o -name "requirements*.txt" -o -name "pyproject.toml" -o -name "package.json" -o -name ".env.example" -o -name...)

  ⎿  total 18

● Bash(cd /d/IISc/Projects/proof-verifier/backend/lean && ls service scripts && grep -n "@app\.\|CORS\|allow_origins" service/*.py | head -20; grep -rni "memory\|RAM\|GB" ../../SPEC/01-lean-infra* 2>/dev/...)
  ⎿  scripts:

● Your instance is launching. What you do next depends on two settings you chose in the launch wizard, because this backend is heavy. The Lean + Mathlib Docker image is about **12.6 GB**, Mathlib needs **several GB of RAM**, and the service takes **1–2+ minutes to start**.

## Step 0: Check the instance type and disk size first

Go to **EC2 → Instances**, click `i-02561c9ea01c01111` and look at:

- **Instance type.** `t2.micro`, `t3.micro` and `t3.small` (1–2 GB RAM) **will not work**: Mathlib will be killed for running out of memory. Under AWS's credit-based free plan you can pick **`m7i-flex.large` (2 vCPU / 8 GB)**, so use that. If yours is smaller, stop the instance and change it under **Actions → Instance settings → Change instance type**.
- **Storage.** The default 8 GB is far too small. You need about **40 GB** (gp3), which covers the build stage, the final image and Docker overhead. If yours is 8 GB, either terminate and relaunch with 40 GB, or go to **Storage → the volume → Modify volume**. After resizing that way you also have to grow the filesystem.
- **Key pair.** Make sure you downloaded the `.pem` file. You need it to SSH in.

## Step 1: Open the ports

Go to **Security tab → the security group → Edit inbound rules** and add:

- SSH (22): source **My IP**. Don't use 0.0.0.0/0.
- HTTP (80) and HTTPS (443): source 0.0.0.0/0. These are for the HTTPS proxy in step 5.

Don't open 8000 to the public.

## Step 2: SSH in

Copy the **Public IPv4 address**, then from PowerShell:
```powershell
ssh -i C:\path\to\your-key.pem ubuntu@<PUBLIC_IP>
```
The username is `ubuntu` on an Ubuntu AMI and `ec2-user` on Amazon Linux. If Windows complains about key permissions, run `icacls your-key.pem /inheritance:r /grant:r "$($env:USERNAME):R"`.

## Step 3: Install Docker and add swap

Swap protects the build from out-of-memory kills.
```bash
sudo apt update && sudo apt install -y docker.io git
sudo usermod -aG docker ubuntu && newgrp docker
sudo fallocate -l 8G /swapfile && sudo chmod 600 /swapfile && sudo mkswap /swapfile && sudo swapon /swapfile
echo '/swapfile none swap sw 0 0' | sudo tee -a /etc/fstab
```

## Step 4: Build and run on the instance

Build on the instance rather than uploading 12.6 GB from your PC, because AWS's download speed for the Mathlib cache is much faster.
```bash
git clone <your-repo-url> proof-verifier
cd proof-verifier/backend/lean
docker build -t lean-svc .          # ~20–40 min
docker run -d --restart unless-stopped --name lean -p 127.0.0.1:8000:8000 \
  -e LEAN_API_KEY='<make-a-long-random-string>' lean-svc
docker logs -f lean                 # wait for startup (~1–2 min)
curl localhost:8000/health
```
`/health` is the only open endpoint. `/start_goal`, `/apply_tactic`, `/check_file` and `/load_sorries` require the key.

## Step 5: Put HTTPS in front with Caddy

Your frontend is on Vercel (HTTPS), and browsers block calls from an HTTPS page to a plain-HTTP backend. Use Caddy with a free sslip.io hostname so you don't need a domain:
```bash
sudo apt install -y caddy
echo '<PUBLIC-IP-with-dashes>.sslip.io {
  reverse_proxy localhost:8000
}' | sudo tee /etc/caddy/Caddyfile
sudo systemctl restart caddy
```
For example, if your IP is `[ip]`, the hostname is `3-110-5-20.sslip.io`. Also allocate an **Elastic IP** (EC2 → Elastic IPs → Allocate → Associate with the instance). Without one, the IP and hostname change every time you stop and start the instance.

## Step 6: Protect your credits

Set up **Billing → Budgets** with an alert at about $10. An `m7i-flex.large` running 24/7 uses roughly $70/month of credit, plus the cost of storage. Stop the instance when you're not demoing.

## Before the app can use this backend

- The frontend still has no real API client. `app/src/api/index.ts` throws for `VITE_API_MODE=real`, so setting `VITE_API_BASE_URL` on Vercel won't do anything yet.
- `service/main.py` has no CORS setup, so browser calls from your Vercel domain will be blocked until it's added.
- Your spec (`SPEC/01-lean-infra.md`) still says the backend runs on Oracle's free tier. Update it once AWS works.

Tell me the instance type and disk size you chose and I'll adjust the steps if needed.

The network part works now, since the server answered. The login was refused, and there are two likely reasons.

**1. The username is probably wrong.** The `gssapi-keyex,gssapi-with-mic` part of the error is what **Amazon Linux** servers print. Ubuntu servers don't. So you probably picked the Amazon Linux image, not Ubuntu, and Amazon Linux's username is `ec2-user`. Try:
```powershell
ssh -i "$HOME\.ssh\proof-verifier-key.pem" ec2-user@[ip]
```
To confirm which image you picked, open the instance's **Details** tab and look at **AMI name**: it starts with `al2023-...` for Amazon Linux or `ubuntu/...` for Ubuntu.

**2. The key pair might not match.** On the same **Details** tab, check that **Key pair assigned at launch** says `proof-verifier-key`. If it shows a different name, the file you have won't work.

**Also, this server is in Sydney.** `54.252.x.x` addresses belong to Sydney (`ap-southeast-2`), not Mumbai, so the instance was probably launched before the region was switched. It will still work, but every request from India takes longer than it would with Mumbai.

**What I'd suggest:** since nothing is installed on this server yet, the cleanest fix is to **terminate it and relaunch** following Part A exactly:
- region set to **Mumbai**
- **Ubuntu 24.04** as the image
- `m7i-flex.large`
- 40 GiB of storage
- `proof-verifier-key` as the key pair. Key pairs belong to one region, so in Mumbai you'll need to create a new key pair.

Then all the commands in my guide work exactly as written.

## Part G: HTTPS

**Before starting,** confirm that the **HTTP (80)** and **HTTPS (443)** inbound rules from earlier are saved in the security group. Caddy can't get a certificate without them.

Amazon Linux has no Caddy package, so you'll run Caddy as a Docker container. Your hostname is `54-252-156-144.sslip.io`.

```bash
cat > ~/Caddyfile <<'EOF'
54-252-156-144.sslip.io {
  reverse_proxy lean:8000
}
EOF
docker run -d --restart unless-stopped --name caddy --network web \
  -p 80:80 -p 443:443 \
  -v ~/Caddyfile:/etc/caddy/Caddyfile -v caddy_data:/data caddy
docker logs caddy
```

The logs should mention `certificate obtained successfully` within about 30 seconds.

**Three things to finish in AWS:**
1. **Elastic IP.** Did you do Part B? If `[ip]` isn't an Elastic IP, it changes the next time you stop and start the instance, and HTTPS stops working because the hostname is based on the IP. Check under **EC2 → Elastic IPs**. If the list is empty, allocate one and associate it with the instance. That gives you a new IP, so tell me what it is and I'll update the Caddyfile commands.
2. **Budget alert.** Search for **Budgets**, then **Create budget → Monthly cost budget**, set **$15**, and enter your email.
3. **Stop the instance when you're not using it** (EC2 → Instance state → Stop). It uses about $70/month of credit if left running 24/7. When you start it again, both containers come back on their own and the backend is ready after about 2 minutes.

**Before the Vercel app can use the backend, the code needs two changes:**
- **CORS in `backend/lean/service/main.py`**, so browsers allow your Vercel domain to call the backend. After this change you'd `git pull` and rebuild on the server, which is quick because only the last layer changes.
- **A real API client in `app/src/api/index.ts`.** There's also a catch: if the frontend calls the backend directly, the `LEAN_API_KEY` ends up inside the browser code, where anyone can read it. The usual fix is a small Vercel serverless function that adds the key on the server side, so the browser never sees it.

Turn 87
should i keep the env variables as secret or config?
Response
Only the key needs to be a secret. The other two are fine as plain config.

| Variable | Type | Why |
|---|---|---|
| `LEAN_API_KEY` | **Secret** (Vercel calls it **Sensitive**) | It's the key that unlocks your Lean server. |
| `LEAN_API_URL` | Config | It's a public address that anyone can already find. |
| `VITE_API_MODE` | Config | It's built into the page anyway: anything starting with `VITE_` ends up in the browser code, so marking it secret wouldn't hide it. |

**In Vercel:** when you add `LEAN_API_KEY`, turn on the **Sensitive** toggle. After you save, Vercel won't show the value again, even to you, so keep your own copy in a password manager or notes app. The function still receives the key normally at runtime, so nothing in the code changes.

For all three variables, tick **Production** and **Preview**, then redeploy.

## 1. Start the backend
1. In AWS, go to **EC2 → Instances → `lean-backend` → Instance state → Start**.
2. Wait about 3 minutes: the machine boots, then Mathlib loads.
3. Open `https://54-252-156-144.sslip.io/health` in your browser. Refresh until it shows `"ready":true`.

## 2. Test the Vercel function directly
Open your deployed site, press **F12**, go to the **Console** tab and paste this:

```js
for (const source of [
  'theorem t : 1 + 1 = 2 := by\n  norm_num\n  all_goals sorry\n',
  'theorem t : 1 + 1 = 2 := by\n  all_goals sorry\n',
  'theorem t : 1 + 1 = 3 := by\n  norm_num\n  all_goals sorry\n',
]) {
  const r = await fetch('/api/lean', {
    method: 'POST',
    headers: { 'Content-Type': 'application/json' },
    body: JSON.stringify({ op: 'check_file', source }),
  })
  console.log(r.status, (await r.json()).status)
}
```

Expected output:
```
200 closed   ← finished proof
200 open     ← nothing proved yet
200 error    ← false statement
```
The first two lines also confirm that the `all_goals sorry` trick works on real Lean.

If you get something else:

| What you see | What it means |
|---|---|
| `500` "not set" | The Vercel env vars are missing on this deployment. Check that they're ticked for this environment (Production or Preview), then redeploy. |
| `502` "not reachable" | EC2 is off or still booting. |
| `503` | Lean is still loading. Wait a minute and try again. |
| `401` | The `LEAN_API_KEY` on Vercel doesn't match the one the container was started with. |
| `404`, or the page's HTML instead of JSON | The `api/` folder wasn't deployed. Check that the Vercel project's root directory is `app`. |

The `all_goals sorry` I add at the end of each proof does nothing once the proof is finished, so Lean warns about it. The backend counts any message containing the word "sorry" as unfinished.

|---|---|
| `1 + 1 = 2` with `norm_num` (finished) | `closed` ✅ |
| `1 + 1 = 2` with no steps (unfinished) | `open`, goal `1 + 1 = 2` ✅ |

| the same, then `simp` | `closed` ✅ |
| an unknown tactic | `error`, with Lean's message ✅ |

Each check took about 0.6–1.5 s end to end. The `ℕ` failure in the first run was my shell garbling the symbol; with the request saved as UTF-8 it works.

Branch new tracks from `integration` so they all inherit `/contracts`.

**5. Merge small pieces into `integration` often.** Rebase or merge `integration` into your track branch regularly. Track 11 (integration) and track 12 (polish) come last.

- `git worktree list` shows what exists.
- `git worktree remove ..\pv-canvas` removes a finished one.

● That's expected — Oracle's Always Free tier still requires a valid card for identity verification at signup, even though Always Free resources themselves never get charged. A few things to know and your options:

**Why it asks:**
- It's anti-abuse/identity verification, not a billing trap. Oracle typically puts a small refundable authorization hold (not a real charge) during signup, then releases it.
- You only get billed if you later explicitly upgrade to a Pay-As-You-Go account — Always Free resources stay free indefinitely without that upgrade.

**What usually trips people up (especially in India):**
- RuPay-only cards are often rejected — it needs a Visa/Mastercard/Amex.
- The card needs **international transactions enabled** (check with your bank/app if this is off by default).
- Debit cards do generally work for Oracle, not just credit cards, as long as the above two are true.

**Your options:**
1. Use a Visa/Mastercard debit card with international transactions enabled — should go through with just the small refundable hold.
2. Ask someone with a qualifying card if you can use theirs just for verification (no actual spend happens on Always Free).
3. If no card is available at all, reconsider hosting — the SPEC's hosting changelog (`SPEC/01-lean-infra.changelog.md`) already evaluated alternatives when Azure got blocked: Render, Fly.io, Koyeb, Northflank, Google Cloud Run, AWS. Worth checking which of those don't require a card for their free tier before committing further to Oracle.

Do you have a debit/credit card you can try, or should I pull up what the SPEC already noted about those alternative providers in case Oracle is a dead end?

Turn 115
can i change the link name?
Response
Yes. On Vercel, the free link is `<project-name>.vercel.app`, and you have two ways to change it.

  **Free `.vercel.app` name:**
  - **Before deploying:** change **Project Name** on the screen you're on (currently `proof-verifier`). That becomes `proof-verifier.vercel.app`, if nobody else has taken it.
  - **After deploying:** go to Project → **Settings → Domains**. Click **Add** and type any free name, for example `proofcheck.vercel.app`. Vercel tells you right away if it's taken. You can then remove the old one or keep both.

  Names can contain only lowercase letters, numbers and hyphens, and they must be unique across all of Vercel. If your name is taken, Vercel adds a random suffix like `proof-verifier-abc123.vercel.app`, and you can fix it later in Domains.

  **Your own domain** (like `proofverifier.in`): buy it from any registrar, add it under **Settings → Domains**, and Vercel shows you the DNS records to set. HTTPS is still free. The spec lists a custom domain as not yet decided, so for now the `.vercel.app` link is enough.

  If you change the link later, remember to update the address the backend services allow (CORS) when they go live.

● Vercel now mentions running Docker containers. Checking whether that works on the free plan, and how big our Lean image is:

● WebFetch(https://vercel.com/kb/guide/does-vercel-support-docker-deployments)
  ⎿  # Container Images on Vercel

● Grep((?i)\d+(\.\d+)?\s?GB.{0,100}(image|olean|mathlib)|(image|mathlib).{0,100}\d+(\.\d+)?\s?GB)
  ⎿  D:\IISc\Projects\proof-verifier\SPEC\09-feedback.md:32:12.6 GB image

● **Short answer: not for the Lean checker, and probably not for recognition either. Keep the backend on Oracle as planned.** Vercel is still useful for one small backend job, described at the end.

  I compared Vercel's current function limits (their docs, updated 2026-08-24) with what our Lean service needs, using the sizes measured in `SPEC/01-lean-infra.changelog.md`.

  | | Lean checker needs | Vercel free plan (Hobby) |
  |---|---|---|
  | Size | **12.6 GB** Docker image, after the shrink work; Mathlib alone is about 5 GB and can't be trimmed further | Max **5 GB**, and that's a beta feature (500 MB normally for Python) |
  | Memory | 4 GB is Lean's default limit; Mathlib work often runs 8–12 GB | **2 GB**, 1 CPU, with no way to raise it on Hobby (Pro gets 4 GB max) |
  | Always-on | Loading Mathlib at startup is slow, so the service wants to stay running | Shuts down after **5 minutes** without traffic, so the next check pays that full startup again |
  | Time per request | Fine | 5 minutes max, which is OK |

  Vercel can now run Docker images, but they're treated like its other functions and hit the same size, memory and 5-minute-idle limits. The image is 2.5× too big and the memory is at least 2× too small, even on the paid plan.

  **Recognition service (track 6):** it isn't built yet, so there's nothing to measure. It could only fit if the model and its libraries stay under the size limit and run inference in 2 GB on one CPU. It would also restart cold after each quiet spell. It's simpler to keep it next to Lean on Oracle, as the spec already decides.

  **Where Vercel would fit:** the spec (`SPEC/03-canvas.md`, open question 14) says to add a "thin proxy" that holds the API key once the app goes public. That's a small function that forwards requests to Oracle so the key isn't visible in the browser. It runs well under Vercel's limits, and because it would share the frontend's address, CORS stops being an issue.

  So the plan stays the same: frontend on Vercel (done), Lean and recognition on Oracle Cloud's free tier, and later maybe that small proxy on Vercel. Nothing in the spec needs to change, since the backend decision is unchanged.

  Sources:
  - [Vercel Functions Limits](https://vercel.com/docs/functions/limitations)
  - [Does Vercel support Docker deployments?](https://vercel.com/kb/guide/does-vercel-support-docker-deployments)

  ## What already works

  - **Canvas controls** change with screen width: at 1280px and 1024px (`canvas.css:104-116`), and on phones (under 730px, or a short landscape screen). On phones the toolbar drops laser, zoom, undo and redo, and sits clear of Excalidraw's bottom bar.
  - **Touch sizes:** on touch screens, toolbar buttons grow to 44px and the shortcut labels are hidden.
  - **Side panel** pushes the canvas aside at 1280px and wider. Below that it slides over the canvas, and on phones it goes full width (`WorkingView.tsx`).
  - **Dialogs** scroll if they're too tall and keep a 16px margin. Help switches to 2 columns on wider screens.
  - **Welcome overlay** hides its secondary text on short landscape screens.
  - **Completed-proof page** keeps a readable width, its buttons wrap, and Lean code scrolls sideways.

  ## Gaps I found
  1. **Ink colour and size buttons stay 36px on touch** (`ProofToolbar.tsx:152,175`). Unlike the toolbar buttons, they have no `pointer-coarse:size-11`, so they're under the 44px touch size the app itself sets. This matters because the pen popover is used most on a tablet.
  2. **The companion's message is heavily cut off on phones.** Its width is capped at 40% of the screen (`CompanionBubble.tsx:185`), about 150px on a phone, so most feedback ends in "…". The message is the main way feedback reaches the user.
  3. **Positioning relies on scattered pixel values.** Toolbar and companion positions use hard-coded numbers (72, 88, 136, 148) in `canvas.css`, plus `top: 72` in `ProofToolbar.tsx:347` and `EDGE`/`PANEL_CLEARANCE` in `CompanionBubble.tsx`. Every new floating control needs another rule for when it clashes with the others.
  4. **Screen-size breakpoints live in five places:**
     - CSS: 1279 and 1023
     - JS `useMedia`: 1280 and 768
     - Excalidraw: 730
     - Tailwind `md`
     - Height: 500

     They're already slightly inconsistent, e.g. 768 in the toolbar vs 1023 in the CSS for the tablet layout.
  5. **Need a real device to confirm:**
     - The app uses `height: 100%` instead of `100dvh`, so on iOS Safari the bottom bar or toolbar may sit under the browser's own bar.
     - The on-screen keyboard may cover the "type this step" dialog.
     - The "Read as X. Correct?" prompt may run off the screen edge when the symbol is near the edge.
  6. **Nothing tests screen sizes automatically**, so regressions will slip in.

  ## Suggested order
  1. Quick fixes: gaps 1 and 2, plus switching to `dvh`. Small changes.
  2. Put the breakpoints in one place (gap 4).
  3. Replace the pixel values with a shared layout for the floating controls (gap 3). This is the bigger change.
  4. Check on a real phone and tablet, or with Playwright screenshots at a few sizes.

  Before we decide, one question: by "responsive", did you mean **layout on different screen sizes** (what I audited) or **speed**, meaning drawing lag and slow feedback? Speed would need a separate check of the drawing and recognition pipeline.

  If you want a live check, connect the Claude Chrome extension and I'll screenshot phone, tablet and desktop sizes.

  **One Excalidraw quirk I had to work around:** Excalidraw doesn't make its undo/redo state available to the app, so the new buttons follow Excalidraw's own hidden undo/redo buttons. Those can be wrong right after a page loads: Excalidraw shows Undo as available on a fresh page, and pressing it does nothing. So the app treats a freshly loaded page as having nothing to undo or redo, which is always true at that point, and follows Excalidraw's buttons from the first change to the drawing onward.

● The app now works on phones and tablets. Laptops look the same as before. I checked each size with screenshots in headless Edge set to act like a touch device, at 390×844 (phone), 844×390 (phone sideways), 820×1180 (tablet upright), 1180×820 (tablet sideways) and 1366×768 (laptop).   **What was broken**
  - **Phones:** Excalidraw switches to its own phone layout below 730px wide, or on short landscape screens. That put its own tools and a style button behind ours. Our toolbar ran off both sides of the screen and covered the Page menu.
  - **Tablet upright:** the toolbar overlapped the Page menu.
  - **Tablet sideways with the step panel open:** the toolbar moved left and covered the Page menu. This was already broken before this change.

  **What changed**
  - **Phone layout** (`canvas.css`): the styles switch on when Excalidraw itself goes into phone mode, so the two always agree.
    - Excalidraw's extra tools and style button are hidden.
    - Its bottom bar stays, with the menu, undo and redo.
    - The Page menu moves into the top-left corner that Excalidraw's menu button leaves empty.
    - The toolbar and companion face sit above the bottom bar.
  - **Phone toolbar** (`ProofToolbar.tsx`): drops the laser pointer, the zoom buttons (pinch to zoom instead) and undo/redo (they're in Excalidraw's bottom bar). The rest fits on one row, even on a 360px screen.
  - **Touch screens:** the small shortcut labels on the toolbar buttons and in the welcome screen are hidden, since there's no keyboard.
  - **Toolbar in general:** it never runs wider than the screen and wraps onto a second row if needed. On screens under 1024px wide it drops below the Page menu row. It only moves aside for the step panel when the screen is at least 768px wide. On a phone the panel covers the whole screen.
  - **Companion face** (`CompanionBubble.tsx`): its distance from the edges now changes with screen size, so it stays clear of the toolbar and the bottom bar.
  - **Welcome screen** (`WelcomeOverlay.tsx`):
    - The "Pick the pen" hint stays on screen and follows the pen when the toolbar moves.
    - On short landscape screens the welcome text and that hint are hidden so nothing overlaps.

  **Not changed:** the step panel, dialogs and the completed-proof page already worked on phones.

● The fixtures test passes now, and so does the rest of the suite (66 of 66).

  **Cause:** the fixture and the contract match; the test just couldn't find the example. With `core.autocrlf=true`, Git checks out `contracts/proof-step.md` on Windows with Windows line endings (`\r\n`). The test looks for ```` ```json ```` followed by `\n`, so it found no JSON example at all. That's why it failed with "expected undefined to be defined". On Linux or Mac checkouts, including CI, it would have passed.

  **Fix:** in `app/src/api/fixtures.test.ts:7`, the regex now accepts either line ending (`\r?\n`), with a comment explaining why.

  If you'd rather fix it for the whole repo, you could add a `.gitattributes` with `*.md text eol=lf`. The test change is enough for now.

  As before, nothing is committed.
